# Colorado

This article is about the U.S. state. For the river, see [Colorado River](180968.md). For the physiographic region, see [Colorado Plateau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colorado_Plateau). For other uses, see [Colorado (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colorado_\(disambiguation\)).

**Colorado**[^cite_note-12] is a landlocked [state](18618239.md) in the [Mountain West](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mountain_states) subregion of the [Western United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_United_States). It is part of the [Southwestern](179571.md) United States, sharing the [Four Corners](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Four_Corners) region with [Arizona](21883824.md), [New Mexico](21649.md), and [Utah](31716.md). It is also bordered by [Wyoming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wyoming) to the north, [Nebraska](21647.md) to the northeast, [Kansas](16716.md) to the east, and [Oklahoma](22489.md) to the southeast. Colorado is noted for its landscape of [mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_mountains_of_the_United_States#Colorado), forests, [high plains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High_Plains_\(United_States\)), mesas, canyons, plateaus, rivers, and desert lands. It encompasses most of the [Southern Rocky Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Rocky_Mountains), as well as the northeastern portion of the [Colorado Plateau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colorado_Plateau) and the western edge of the [Great Plains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Plains). Colorado is the [eighth-largest U.S. state by area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_U.S._states_and_territories_by_area) and the [20th by population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_U.S._states_and_territories_by_population). The [United States Census Bureau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Census_Bureau) estimated the population of Colorado to be 5,957,493 as of July 1, 2024, a 3.2% increase from the [2020 United States census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_United_States_census).[^cite_note-census.gov-13]

The region has been inhabited by [Native Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_Americans_in_the_United_States) and their [Paleo-Indian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paleo-Indian) ancestors for at least 13,500 years and possibly much longer. The eastern edge of the [Rocky Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rocky_Mountains) was a major migration route for early peoples who spread throughout the Americas. In 1848, much of the [Nuevo México](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Fe_de_Nuevo_M%C3%A9xico) region was annexed to the United States with the [Treaty of Guadalupe Hidalgo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Guadalupe_Hidalgo). The [Pike's Peak Gold Rush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pike's_Peak_Gold_Rush) of 1858–1862 created an influx of settlers. On February 28, 1861, [U.S. president](24113.md) [James Buchanan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Buchanan) signed [an act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colorado_Organic_Act) creating the [Territory of Colorado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Territory_of_Colorado),[^cite_note-ColoradoOrganicAct-2] and on August 1, 1876, president [Ulysses S. Grant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ulysses_S._Grant) signed [Proclamation 230](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colorado_Statehood_Proclamation), admitting Colorado to the [Union](3434750.md) as the 38th state.[^cite_note-ColoradoStatehoodProclamation-3]

[Denver](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Denver) is the capital, the [most populous city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colorado_municipalities_by_population), and the center of the [Front Range Urban Corridor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Front_Range_Urban_Corridor). [Colorado Springs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colorado_Springs,_Colorado) is the second-most populous city of the state. Residents of the state are known as [Coloradans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_people_from_Colorado), although the antiquated "Coloradoan" is occasionally used.[^cite_note-statestyle-14][^cite_note-Coloradan-15] Colorado generally ranks as one of the top U.S. states for education attainment, employment, and healthcare quality.[^cite_note-CensusData-16][^cite_note-\:0-17][^cite_note-18] Major parts of [its economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_Colorado) include government and defense, mining, [agriculture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_Colorado#Agriculture), tourism, and manufacturing. With increasing temperatures and decreasing water availability, Colorado's agriculture, forestry, and tourism economies are [expected to be heavily affected by climate change](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_change_in_Colorado).[^cite_note-CC-19]

## History

Main articles: [Prehistory of Colorado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prehistory_of_Colorado) and [History of Colorado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Colorado)

For a chronological guide, see [Timeline of Colorado history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_Colorado_history).

The region that is today the State of Colorado has been inhabited by [Native Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_peoples_of_the_Americas) and their [Paleo-Indian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paleo-Indian) ancestors for at least 13,500 years and possibly more than 37,000 years.[^cite_note-Footprints-20][^cite_note-Mammoth-21] The [Lindenmeier site](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lindenmeier_site) contains artifacts dating from approximately 8720 BCE. The [Ancient Pueblo peoples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Pueblo_peoples) lived in the valleys and mesas of the [Colorado Plateau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colorado_Plateau) in far southwestern Colorado.[^cite_note-22] The [Ute Nation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ute_Nation) inhabited the mountain valleys of the [Southern Rocky Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Rocky_Mountains) and the [Western Rocky Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Rocky_Mountains), even as far east as the Front Range of the present day. The [Apache](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apache) and the [Comanche](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comanche) also inhabited the Eastern and Southeastern parts of the state. In the 17th century, the [Arapaho](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arapaho_Nation) and [Cheyenne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cheyenne_Nation) moved west from the [Great Lakes region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Lakes_region) to hunt across the [High Plains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High_Plains_\(United_States\)) of Colorado and [Wyoming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wyoming).[^cite_note-23][^cite_note-24][^cite_note-25]

The U.S. acquired the territorial claim to the eastern Rocky Mountains with the [Louisiana Purchase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisiana_Purchase) from France in 1803. In 1806, [Zebulon Pike](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zebulon_Pike) led a [U.S. Army reconnaissance expedition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pike_Expedition) into the disputed region. Colonel Pike and his troops were arrested by Spanish cavalrymen in the [San Luis Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Luis_Valley) the following February, taken to [Chihuahua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chihuahua,_Chihuahua), and expelled from Mexico the following July.[^cite_note-26][^cite_note-\:1-27]

Under the 1819 [Adams-Onís Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adams-On%C3%ADs_Treaty), the U.S. acquired Florida from Spain while relinquishing claims to lands west of the [100th meridian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/100th_meridian_west) and south of both the [Arkansas River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arkansas_River) and the [42nd parallel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/42nd_parallel_north). The treaty took effect on February 22, 1821. Having settled its border with Spain, the U.S. admitted the southeastern portion of the [Territory of Missouri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Missouri_Territory) to the Union as the [state of Missouri](19571.md) on August 10, 1821. The remainder of Missouri Territory, including what would become northeastern Colorado, became an unorganized territory and remained so for 33 years over the [question of slavery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavery_in_the_United_States). After 11 years of war, Spain finally recognized the independence of Mexico with the [Treaty of Córdoba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_C%C3%B3rdoba) signed on August 24, 1821. Mexico eventually ratified the Adams–Onís Treaty in 1831. The [Texian Revolt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texian_Revolt) of 1835–36 fomented a dispute between the U.S. and Mexico which eventually erupted into the [Mexican–American War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexican%E2%80%93American_War) in 1846. Mexico surrendered its northern territory to the U.S. with the [Treaty of Guadalupe Hidalgo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Guadalupe_Hidalgo) after the war in 1848; this included much of the western and southern areas of Colorado.



Map of the [Mexican Cession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexican_Cession), with the white representing the territory the United States received from Mexico (plus land ceded to the [Republic of Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_Texas)) after the [Mexican–American War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexican%E2%80%93American_War). Well over half of Colorado was received from this treaty.

The [Santa Fe Trail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Fe_Trail) connected the [Missouri River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Missouri_River) to [Santa Fe, New Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Fe,_New_Mexico), where it linked with the [Camino Real de Tierra Adentro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camino_Real_de_Tierra_Adentro) extending southward into Mexico. Others traveling overland avoided the rugged [Southern Rocky Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Rocky_Mountains), and instead followed the [North Platte River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Platte_River) and [Sweetwater River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sweetwater_River_\(Wyoming\)) to [South Pass (Wyoming)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Pass_\(Wyoming\)), the lowest crossing of the [Continental Divide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continental_Divide) between the Southern Rocky Mountains and the Central Rocky Mountains. In 1849, the Mormons of the Salt Lake Valley organized the extralegal [State of Deseret](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_of_Deseret), claiming the entire [Great Basin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Basin) and all lands drained by the rivers [Green](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_River_\(Colorado_River\)), [Grand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_River_\(Colorado\)), and [Colorado](180968.md). The federal government of the U.S. flatly refused to recognize the new Mormon government because it was [theocratic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_The_Church_of_Jesus_Christ_of_Latter-day_Saints#Brigham_Young's_early_theocratic_leadership) and [sanctioned](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanctions_\(law\)) [plural marriage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mormonism_and_polygamy). Instead, the [Compromise of 1850](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Compromise_of_1850) divided the [Mexican Cession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexican_Cession) and the northwestern claims of Texas into a new state and two new territories, the [state of California](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_of_California), the [Territory of New Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Territory_of_New_Mexico), and the [Territory of Utah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Territory_of_Utah). On April 9, 1851, [Hispano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hispanos_of_New_Mexico) settlers from the area of [Taos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taos,_New_Mexico) settled the village of [San Luis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Luis,_Colorado), then in the [New Mexico Territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Mexico_Territory), as Colorado's first permanent [Euro-American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_American) [settlement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Settlement_\(migration\)), further cementing the traditions of [New Mexican cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Mexican_cuisine) and [New Mexico music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Mexico_music) in the developing [Southern Rocky Mountain Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Rocky_Mountain_Front).[^cite_note-Lyons_2018-28][^cite_note-Sexton_2019-29]

These paragraphs are an excerpt from [Jefferson Territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jefferson_Territory).\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Jefferson_Territory\&action=edit)]

The [Provisional Government of the Territory of Jefferson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jefferson_Territory) was an extralegal and unrecognized [United States territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_territory) that existed in the [Pike's Peak mining region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pike's_Peak_Gold_Rush) from October 24, 1859, until it yielded to the new [Territory of Colorado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Territory_of_Colorado) on June 6, 1861.[^cite_note-Jefferson_Territory_Jefferson_Territory-30] The Jefferson Territory included all of the present [State of Colorado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_of_Colorado) and portions of the present [states](18618239.md) of [Kansas](16716.md), [Nebraska](21647.md), [Utah](31716.md), and [Wyoming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wyoming). The people were desirous of a closer government than those five territories provided.

The government of the Jefferson Territory, while democratically elected, was never legally recognized by the [United States government](195149.md), although it managed the territory with relatively free rein for 19 months. Many of the laws enacted by the General Assembly of the Territory of Jefferson were reenacted and given official sanction by the new [Colorado General Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colorado_General_Assembly) in 1861.

### Colorado Territory

These paragraphs are an excerpt from [Colorado Territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colorado_Territory).\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Colorado_Territory\&action=edit)]

The [Territory of Colorado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colorado_Territory) was an [organized incorporated territory of the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organized_incorporated_territory_of_the_United_States) that existed from February 28, 1861,[^cite_note-Colorado_Territory_Colorado_Origin_Act-31] until August 1, 1876, when it was admitted to the [Union](3434750.md) as the 38th State of Colorado.[^cite_note-Colorado_Territory_Colorado_Statehood_Proclamation-32] The territory was created during the onset of the [American Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Civil_War) in the wake of the [Pike's Peak Gold Rush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pike's_Peak_Gold_Rush), which brought the first large concentration of settlers from the east to the region. The war stifled growth, but following the war mining grew more industrialized and fueled the economy. The construction of railroads further improved the economy. The boundaries of the newly designated Colorado Territory were very similar to those of the modern State of Colorado.



The territories of [New Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Mexico_Territory), [Utah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Utah_Territory), [Kansas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kansas_Territory), and [Nebraska](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nebraska_Territory) before the creation of the [Territory of Colorado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Territory_of_Colorado)

#### Civil war

Main article: [Colorado Territory in the American Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colorado_Territory_in_the_American_Civil_War)

In 1861 following the beginning of the [American Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Civil_War), there were several small disputes and skirmishes between confederate supporters and union supporters. In August, Governor Gilpin organized the [1st Colorado Infantry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1st_Colorado_Infantry). [John P. Slough](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_P._Slough) led the group to [New Mexico Territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Mexico_Territory) in February–March 1862. There they fought in the battles of [Apache Canyon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Apache_Canyon), [Glorieta Pass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Glorieta_Pass) and [Peralta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Peralta). Slough resigned in April 1862 and was replaced by Major [John M. Chivington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_M._Chivington).[^cite_note-33]



John Chivington, commander of the 3rd Colorado Cavalry at the Sand Creek Massacre.

With resources tied up in the war there was little left over for mines, farms, and infrastructure, and Denver stagnated.[^cite_note-MiningCampto-34][^cite_note-35]

On March 26, 1862 [John Evans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Evans_\(Colorado_governor\)) was appointed by Abraham Lincoln as the second governor of the territory.[^cite_note-HC-36] He prompted investment in Denver rail projects,[^cite_note-HC-36][^cite_note-kd1-37]<sup>: 85 </sup> and founded the [University of Denver](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Denver) in 1864.[^cite_note-38]<sup>: 22 </sup>



John Evans with daughter Josephine, c. 1859

Anti-native sentiment grew during the Civil War years. Exterminationist ideology, led in Colorado by Evans and Chivington, advocated for the total eradication of all native people in the territory.[^cite_note-39] The [Colorado War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colorado_War) worsened relations with natives until 1864 when Chivington ordered the [Sand Creek Massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sand_Creek_Massacre) and Evans was forced to resign the following spring.[^cite_note-40]<sup>: 85 </sup>

In 1867, the [Union Pacific Railroad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_Pacific_Railroad) laid its tracks west to Weir, now [Julesburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Julesburg,_Colorado), in the northeast corner of the Territory. The Union Pacific linked up with the [Central Pacific Railroad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Pacific_Railroad) at [Promontory Summit, Utah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Promontory_Summit,_Utah), on May 10, 1869, to form the [First transcontinental railroad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_transcontinental_railroad). The [Denver Pacific Railway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Denver_Pacific_Railway) reached Denver in June of the following year, and the [Kansas Pacific](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kansas_Pacific) arrived two months later to forge the second line across the continent.[^cite_note-r813-41]



The Sand Creek Massacre occurred in 1864 during the [American Indian Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Indian_Wars) when the U.S. Army faught Native Americans

### Statehood

Main articles: [Admission to the Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Admission_to_the_Union), [List of U.S. states by date of admission to the Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_U.S._states_by_date_of_admission_to_the_Union), [Colorado Silver Boom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colorado_Silver_Boom), and [Cripple Creek Gold Rush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cripple_Creek_Gold_Rush)



The [Georgetown Loop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgetown_Loop) of the [Colorado Central Railroad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colorado_Central_Railroad) as photographed by [William Henry Jackson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Henry_Jackson) in 1899

The [United States Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forty-third_United_States_Congress) passed an enabling act on March 3, 1875, specifying the requirements for the Territory of Colorado to become a state.[^cite_note-ColoradoEnablingAct-42] On August 1, 1876 (four weeks after the [Centennial of the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centennial_of_the_United_States)), U.S. President Ulysses S. Grant signed a proclamation admitting Colorado to the Union as the 38th state and earning it the moniker "Centennial State".[^cite_note-ColoradoStatehoodProclamation-3]

The discovery of a major silver lode near [Leadville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leadville,_Colorado) in 1878 triggered the [Colorado Silver Boom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colorado_Silver_Boom). The [Sherman Silver Purchase Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sherman_Silver_Purchase_Act) of 1890 invigorated silver mining, and Colorado's last, but greatest, gold strike at [Cripple Creek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cripple_Creek,_Colorado) a few months later lured a new generation of gold seekers. Colorado women were granted the right to vote on November 7, 1893, making Colorado the second state to grant [universal suffrage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_suffrage) and the first one by a [popular vote](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1893_Colorado_women's_suffrage_referendum) (of Colorado men). The repeal of the Sherman Silver Purchase Act in 1893 led to a staggering collapse of the mining and agricultural economy of Colorado, but the state slowly and steadily recovered. Between the 1880s and 1930s, Denver's floriculture industry developed into a major industry in Colorado.[^cite_note-43][^cite_note-44] This period became known locally as the [Carnation Gold Rush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carnation_Gold_Rush).[^cite_note-45]

### Twentieth and twenty-first centuries



16th Street in Denver in 1912



The ruins of the [Ludlow Colony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ludlow_massacre) in the aftermath of the 1914 massacre.

Poor labor conditions and discontent among miners resulted in several major clashes between strikers and the [Colorado National Guard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colorado_Army_National_Guard), including the [1903–1904 Western Federation of Miners Strike](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colorado_Labor_Wars) and [Colorado Coalfield War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colorado_Coalfield_War), the latter of which included the [Ludlow massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ludlow_massacre) that killed a dozen women and children.[^cite_note-Philip_Ross_1969-46][^cite_note-47] Both the 1913–1914 Coalfield War and the [Denver streetcar strike of 1920](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Denver_streetcar_strike_of_1920) resulted in federal troops intervening to end the violence.[^cite_note-48] In 1927, the [1927-28 Colorado coal strike](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1927%E2%80%931928_Colorado_Coal_Strike) occurred and was ultimately successful in winning a dollar a day increase in wages.[^cite_note-49][^cite_note-50] During it however the [Columbine Mine massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Columbine_Mine_massacre) resulted in six dead strikers following a confrontation with [Colorado Rangers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colorado_Rangers).[^cite_note-51][^cite_note-52] In a separate incident in [Trinidad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trinidad,_Colorado) the mayor was accused of deputizing members of the KKK against the striking workers.[^cite_note-53] More than 5,000 Colorado miners—many immigrants—are estimated to have died in accidents since records were first formally collected following an 1884 accident in [Crested Butte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crested_Butte,_Colorado) that killed 59.[^cite_note-54]

In 1924, the [Ku Klux Klan](16779.md) Colorado Realm achieved dominance in Colorado politics. With peak membership levels, the [Second Klan](16779.md#Second_Klan) levied significant control over both the local and state [Democrat](5043544.md) and [Republican parties](32070.md), particularly in the governor's office and city governments of Denver, [Cañon City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canon_City,_Colorado), and [Durango](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Durango,_Colorado). A particularly strong element of the Klan controlled the [Denver Police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Denver_Police_Department).[^cite_note-KKK-55] [Cross burnings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cross_burning) became semi-regular occurrences in cities such as [Florence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florence,_Colorado) and Pueblo. The Klan targeted African-Americans, [Catholics](606848.md), Eastern European immigrants, and other non-White [Protestant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestant) groups.[^cite_note-56] Efforts by non-Klan lawmen and lawyers including [Philip Van Cise](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philip_S._Van_Cise) led to a rapid decline in the organization's power, with membership waning significantly by the end of the 1920s.[^cite_note-KKK-55]



Three [10th Mountain Division](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/10th_Mountain_Division) skitroopers above [Camp Hale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camp_Hale) in February 1944

Colorado became the first western state to host a major political convention when the [Democratic Party](5043544.md) met in Denver in 1908.[^cite_note-57] By the [U.S. census in 1930](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Census,_1930), the population of Colorado first exceeded one million residents.[^cite_note-58] Colorado suffered greatly through the [Great Depression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Depression) and the [Dust Bowl](59749.md) of the 1930s, but a major wave of immigration following [World War II](32927.md) boosted Colorado's fortune.[^cite_note-\:2-59] Tourism became a mainstay of the state economy, and high technology became an important economic engine.[^cite_note-\:2-59] The [United States Census Bureau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Census_Bureau) estimated that the population of Colorado exceeded five million in 2009.[^cite_note-60]

On September 11, 1957, a plutonium fire occurred at the [Rocky Flats Plant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rocky_Flats_Plant), which resulted in the significant [plutonium contamination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radioactive_contamination_from_the_Rocky_Flats_Plant) of surrounding populated areas.[^cite_note-61]



Skiers on [Aspen Mountain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aspen_Mountain_\(ski_area\)) in 1961

From the 1940s and 1970s, many protest movements gained momentum in Colorado, predominantly in Denver. This included the [Chicano Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicano_Movement), a [civil rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_rights_movements), and social movement of [Mexican Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexican_Americans) emphasizing a [Chicano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicano) identity that is widely considered to have begun in Denver.[^cite_note-62] The National [Chicano Youth Liberation Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicano_Youth_Liberation_Conference) was held in Colorado in March 1969.[^cite_note-63]

In 1967, Colorado was the first state to loosen restrictions on [abortion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abortion_in_Colorado) when governor [John Love](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Arthur_Love) signed a law allowing abortions in cases of rape, incest, or threats to the woman's mental or physical health. Many states followed Colorado's lead in loosening abortion laws in the 1960s and 1970s.[^cite_note-64]

Since the late 1990s, Colorado has been the site of [multiple major](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_shootings_in_Colorado) [mass shootings](31883778.md), including the [Columbine High School massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Columbine_High_School_massacre) in 1999, where high school senior students [Eric Harris and Dylan Klebold](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eric_Harris_and_Dylan_Klebold) killed 13[^cite_note-66] students and one teacher, before committing suicide. The incident made international news, and has since spawned many [copycat incidents](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Copycat_crime), known as the [Columbine effect](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Columbine_effect).[^cite_note-67] On July 20, 2012, a [gunman killed 12 people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_Aurora_theater_shooting) in a movie theater in [Aurora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aurora,_Colorado). The state responded with tighter restrictions on firearms, including [introducing a limit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High-capacity_magazine) on [magazine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magazine_\(firearms\)) capacity.[^cite_note-68] On March 22, 2021, a [gunman killed 10 people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Boulder_shooting), including a police officer, in a [King Soopers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_Soopers) supermarket in [Boulder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boulder,_Colorado).[^cite_note-69] In an instance of [anti-LGBT violence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Violence_against_LGBT_people), a [gunman killed 5 people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colorado_Springs_nightclub_shooting) at a nightclub in [Colorado Springs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colorado_Springs,_Colorado) during the night of November 19–20, 2022.[^cite_note-70]

Four warships of the [U.S. Navy](20518076.md) have been named the [USS *Colorado*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USS_Colorado). The first USS *Colorado* was named for the Colorado River and served in the Civil War and later the [Asiatic Squadron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asiatic_Squadron), where it was attacked during the 1871 [Korean Expedition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_expedition_to_Korea). The later three ships were named in honor of the state, including [an armored cruiser](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USS_Colorado_\(ACR-7\)) and the [battleship USS *Colorado*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USS_Colorado_\(BB-45\)), the latter of which was the lead ship of [her class](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colorado-class_battleship) and served in [World War II in the Pacific](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_the_Pacific) beginning in 1941. At the time of the [attack on Pearl Harbor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attack_on_Pearl_Harbor), the battleship USS *Colorado* was located at the naval base in San Diego, California, and thus went unscathed. The most recent vessel to bear the name USS *Colorado* is [Virginia-class submarine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virginia-class_submarine) [USS *Colorado* (SSN-788)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USS_Colorado_\(SSN-788\)), which was commissioned in 2018.[^cite_note-71]

## Geography

Main article: [Geography of Colorado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_Colorado)

See also: [List of cities and towns in Colorado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_cities_and_towns_in_Colorado), [List of counties in Colorado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_counties_in_Colorado), [List of rivers of Colorado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_rivers_of_Colorado), and [Four Corners Monument](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Four_Corners_Monument)



Colorado is notable for its diverse geography, which includes alpine mountains, high plains, deserts with huge sand dunes, and deep canyons. In 1861, the [United States Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thirty-sixth_United_States_Congress) defined the boundaries of the new [Territory of Colorado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Territory_of_Colorado) exclusively by lines of [latitude](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latitude) and [longitude](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Longitude), stretching from [37°N](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/37th_parallel_north) to [41°N](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/41st_parallel_north) latitude, and from [102°02′48″W](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/25th_meridian_west_from_Washington) to [109°02′48″W](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/32nd_meridian_west_from_Washington) longitude ([25°W](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/25th_meridian_west_from_Washington) to [32°W](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/32nd_meridian_west_from_Washington) from the [Washington Meridian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Meridian)).[^cite_note-ColoradoOrganicAct-2] After 165 years of government surveys, the borders of Colorado were officially defined by 697 [boundary markers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boundary_marker) and 697 [straight boundary lines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Border).[^cite_note-FM-72] Colorado, [Wyoming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wyoming), and [Utah](31716.md) are the only states that have their borders defined solely by straight boundary lines with no natural features.[^cite_note-BT-73] The southwest corner of Colorado is the [Four Corners Monument](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Four_Corners_Monument) at [36°59′56″N](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/37th_parallel_north), [109°2′43″W](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/32nd_meridian_west_from_Washington).[^cite_note-FourCorners-74][^cite_note-FourCornersMonument-75] The [Four Corners Monument](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Four_Corners_Monument), located at the place where Colorado, [New Mexico](21649.md), [Arizona](21883824.md), and [Utah](31716.md) meet, is the only place in the United States where four states meet.[^cite_note-BT-73]

### Plains



The arid high plains in Southeastern Colorado

Approximately half of Colorado is flat and rolling land. East of the Rocky Mountains is the [Colorado Eastern Plains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colorado_Eastern_Plains) of the [High Plains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High_Plains_\(United_States\)), the section of the Great Plains within Colorado at elevations ranging from roughly 3,350 to 7,500 feet (1,020 to 2,290 m).[^cite_note-76] The Colorado plains are mostly prairies but also include [deciduous forests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deciduous_forests), [buttes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Butte), and canyons. Precipitation averages 15 to 25 inches (380 to 640 mm) annually.[^cite_note-ccc-77]

Eastern Colorado is presently mainly farmland and rangeland, along with small farming villages and towns. [Corn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maize), [wheat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wheat), [hay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hay), [soybeans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soybean), and [oats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oat) are all typical crops. Most villages and towns in this region boast both a water tower and a [grain elevator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grain_elevator). Irrigation water is available from both surface and subterranean sources. Surface water sources include the [South Platte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Platte), the [Arkansas River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arkansas_River), and a few other streams. Subterranean water is generally accessed through [artesian wells](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Artesian_well). Heavy usage of these wells for irrigation purposes caused underground water reserves to decline in the region. Eastern Colorado also hosts a considerable amount and range of livestock, such as cattle ranches and hog farms.[^cite_note-78]

### Front Range



[Front Range](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Front_Range) Peaks west of Denver

About 84% of Colorado's population resides along the eastern edge of the Rocky Mountains in the [Front Range Urban Corridor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Front_Range_Urban_Corridor) between [Cheyenne, Wyoming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cheyenne,_Wyoming), and [Pueblo, Colorado](24158.md).[^cite_note-79][^cite_note-\:3-80] This region is partially protected from prevailing storms that blow in from the Pacific Ocean region by the high Rockies in the middle of Colorado.[^cite_note-81] The "Front Range" includes Denver, [Boulder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boulder,_Colorado), [Fort Collins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fort_Collins,_Colorado), [Loveland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loveland,_Colorado), [Castle Rock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Castle_Rock,_Colorado), [Colorado Springs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colorado_Springs,_Colorado), Pueblo, [Greeley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greeley,_Colorado), and other townships and municipalities in between.[^cite_note-\:3-80][^cite_note-82] On the other side of the Rockies, the significant population centers in western Colorado (which is known as "The Western Slope") are the cities of [Grand Junction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Junction,_Colorado), [Durango](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Durango,_Colorado), and [Montrose](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montrose,_Colorado).[^cite_note-\:6-83]

### Mountains

**Map this section's coordinates in "List of mountain peaks of Colorado" using** [OpenStreetMap](https://tools.wmflabs.org/osm4wiki/cgi-bin/wiki/wiki-osm.pl?project=en\&article=List+of+mountain+peaks+of+Colorado\&section=Highest+major+summits)&#x20;

**Download coordinates as:**

- [KML](https://tools.wmflabs.org/kmlexport?article=List+of+mountain+peaks+of+Colorado\&section=Highest+major+summits)

- [GPX (all coordinates)](https://geoexport.toolforge.org/gpx?project=en.wikipedia.org\&coprimary=all\&titles=List+of+mountain+peaks+of+Colorado\&section=Highest+major+summits)

- [GPX (primary coordinates)](https://geoexport.toolforge.org/gpx?project=en.wikipedia.org\&coprimary=primary\&titles=List+of+mountain+peaks+of+Colorado\&section=Highest+major+summits)

- [GPX (secondary coordinates)](https://geoexport.toolforge.org/gpx?project=en.wikipedia.org\&coprimary=secondary\&titles=List+of+mountain+peaks+of+Colorado\&section=Highest+major+summits)

See also: [List of mountain peaks of Colorado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_mountain_peaks_of_Colorado)



[Mount Elbert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Elbert) at 14,440 feet (4,401.2 m) is the highest summit of the [Rocky Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rocky_Mountains) and Colorado.

To the west of the Great Plains of Colorado rises the eastern slope of the [Rocky Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rocky_Mountains). Notable peaks of the Rocky Mountains include [Longs Peak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Longs_Peak), [Mount Blue Sky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Blue_Sky), [Pikes Peak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pikes_Peak), and the [Spanish Peaks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Peaks) near [Walsenburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walsenburg,_Colorado), in southern Colorado.[^cite_note-84] This area drains to the east and the southeast, ultimately either via the [Mississippi River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mississippi_River) or the [Rio Grande](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rio_Grande) into the [Gulf of Mexico](21076367.md).[^cite_note-85]

The Rocky Mountains within Colorado contain 53 true peaks and 58 named peaks[^cite_note-86] that are 14,000 feet (4,267 m) or higher in elevation above sea level, known as [fourteeners](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colorado_fourteeners).[^cite_note-87] These mountains are largely covered with trees such as [conifers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conifer) and [aspens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aspen) up to the [tree line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tree_line), at an elevation of about 12,000 feet (3,658 m) in southern Colorado to about 10,500 feet (3,200 m) in northern Colorado. Above this tree line, only alpine vegetation grows.[^cite_note-88]

Much of the alpine snow melts by mid-August except for a few snow-capped peaks and a few small glaciers. The [Colorado Mineral Belt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colorado_Mineral_Belt), stretching from the [San Juan Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Juan_Mountains) in the southwest to [Boulder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boulder,_Colorado) and [Central City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_City,_Colorado) on the front range, contains most of the historic gold- and silver-mining districts of Colorado.[^cite_note-89] The 30 [highest major summits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Highest_mountain_peaks_of_the_Rocky_Mountains) of the Rocky Mountains of North America are all within the state.[^cite_note-90]

The summit of Mount Elbert at 14,437.6 feet (4,400.58 m) elevation in [Lake County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_County,_Colorado) is the highest point in Colorado and the Rocky Mountains of North America.[^cite_note-Mount_Elbert-4][^cite_note-91] Colorado is the only U.S. state that lies entirely above 1,000 meters elevation. The point where the [Arikaree River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arikaree_River) flows out of [Yuma County, Colorado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yuma_County,_Colorado), and into [Cheyenne County, Kansas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cheyenne_County,_Kansas), is the lowest in Colorado at 3,317 feet (1,011 m) elevation. This point, which is the highest low elevation point of any state,[^cite_note-USGS-5][^cite_note-92] is higher than the high elevation points of 18 states and the District of Columbia.[^cite_note-93]

#### Continental Divide



[Grays Peak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grays_Peak), at 14,278 feet (4,352 m), is the highest point on the [Continental Divide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continental_Divide_of_the_Americas) in North America.

The [Continental Divide of the Americas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continental_Divide_of_the_Americas) extends along the most of the crest of the Rocky Mountains.[^cite_note-94] The area of Colorado to the west of the Continental Divide is called the [Western Slope of Colorado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colorado_Western_Slope).[^cite_note-\:6-83] West of the Continental Divide, water flows to the southwest via the [Colorado River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colorado_River_\(U.S.\)) and the [Green River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_River_\(Colorado_River\)) towards the [Gulf of California](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_of_California).[^cite_note-95]

Within the interior of the Rocky Mountains are several large parks which are high broad basins. In the north, on the east side of the Continental Divide is the [North Park of Colorado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Park_\(Colorado_basin\)). The North Park is drained by the [North Platte River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Platte_River), which flows north into Wyoming and Nebraska. Just to the south of North Park, but on the western side of the Continental Divide, is the [Middle Park of Colorado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Park_\(Colorado_basin\)), which is drained by the Colorado River. The [South Park of Colorado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Park_\(Park_County,_Colorado\)) is the region of the [headwaters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Headwater) of the South Platte River.

### South Central region



The high desert lands that make up the San Luis Valley in Southern Colorado

In south-central Colorado is the large [San Luis Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Luis_Valley), where the headwaters of the [Rio Grande](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rio_Grande) are located. The northern part of the valley is the [San Luis Closed Basin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Luis_Closed_Basin), an [endorheic basin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Endorheic_basin) that helped created the [Great Sand Dunes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Sand_Dunes_National_Park_and_Preserve). The valley sits between the [Sangre de Cristo Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sangre_de_Cristo_Mountains) and [San Juan Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Juan_Mountains). The Rio Grande drains due south into New Mexico, Texas, and Mexico. Across the [Sangre de Cristo Range](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sangre_de_Cristo_Range) to the east of the San Luis Valley lies the [Wet Mountain Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wet_Mountain_Valley). These basins, particularly the San Luis Valley, lie along the [Rio Grande rift](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rio_Grande_rift), a major [geological formation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geological_formation) of the Rocky Mountains, and its branches.

### Western Slope

Main article: [Western Slope of Colorado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Slope_of_Colorado)



[Maroon Bells](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maroon_Bells), at 14,163 ft (4,317 m), is part of [White River National Forest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_River_National_Forest) and a tourist destination



The Colorado National Monument near Grand Junction is made up of high desert canyons and sandstone rock formations.

The Western Slope of Colorado includes the western face of the [Rocky Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rocky_Mountains) and all of the area to the western border. This area includes several terrains and climates from alpine mountains to arid deserts. The Western Slope includes many ski resort towns in the Rocky Mountains and towns west to [Utah](31716.md). It is less populous than the [Front Range](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Front_Range) but includes a large number of national parks and monuments.[^cite_note-96]

The northwestern corner of Colorado is a sparsely populated region,[^cite_note-auto-97][^cite_note-98] and it contains part of the noted [Dinosaur National Monument](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dinosaur_National_Monument), which not only is a [paleontological](23084.md) area, but is also a scenic area of rocky hills, canyons, arid desert, and streambeds.[^cite_note-99][^cite_note-\:4-100] Here, the Green River briefly crosses over into Colorado.[^cite_note-\:4-100][^cite_note-101]

The Western Slope of Colorado is drained by the Colorado River and its tributaries (primarily the [Gunnison River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gunnison_River), [Green River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_River_\(Colorado_River\)), and the [San Juan River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Juan_River_\(Colorado_River\))). The Colorado River flows through [Glenwood Canyon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glenwood_Canyon), and then through an arid valley made up of desert from [Rifle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rifle,_Colorado) to [Parachute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parachute,_Colorado), through the desert canyon of [De Beque Canyon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/De_Beque_Canyon), and into the arid desert of [Grand Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Valley_\(Colorado-Utah\)), where the city of Grand Junction is located.[^cite_note-102]<sup>,</sup>[^cite_note-103]

Also prominent is the [Grand Mesa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Mesa), which lies to the southeast of Grand Junction; the high San Juan Mountains, a rugged mountain range; and to the north and west of the San Juan Mountains, the [Colorado Plateau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colorado_Plateau).

[Grand Junction, Colorado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Junction,_Colorado), at the confluence of the Colorado and Gunnison Rivers, is the largest city on the Western Slope. Grand Junction and Durango are the only major centers of [television broadcasting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Television_broadcasting) west of the Continental Divide in Colorado, though most mountain resort communities publish daily newspapers. Grand Junction is located at the juncture of [Interstate 70](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_70_in_Colorado) and US 50, the only major highways in western Colorado. Grand Junction is also along the major railroad of the Western Slope, the [Union Pacific](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_Pacific). This railroad also provides the tracks for [Amtrak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amtrak)'s *[California Zephyr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/California_Zephyr)* passenger train, which crosses the Rocky Mountains between Denver and Grand Junction.[^cite_note-104]

The Western Slope includes multiple notable destinations in the Colorado Rocky Mountains, including [Glenwood Springs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glenwood_Springs,_Colorado), with its resort hot springs, and the [ski resorts](179821.md) of [Aspen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aspen,_Colorado), [Breckenridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Breckenridge,_Colorado), [Vail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vail,_Colorado), [Crested Butte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crested_Butte,_Colorado), [Steamboat Springs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steamboat_Springs,_Colorado), and [Telluride](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Telluride,_Colorado).[^cite_note-105][^cite_note-106]

Higher education in western and southwestern Colorado includes Colorado Mesa University in Grand Junction,[^cite_note-107] Western Colorado University in Gunnison,[^cite_note-108] Fort Lewis College in Durango,[^cite_note-109] and Colorado Mountain College campuses in Glenwood Springs and Steamboat Springs.[^cite_note-110]

The [Four Corners Monument](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Four_Corners_Monument) in the southwest corner of Colorado marks the common boundary of Colorado, [New Mexico](21649.md), [Arizona](21883824.md), and [Utah](31716.md), the only such place in the United States.[^cite_note-111]

## Climate



[Köppen climate types](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification) of Colorado, using 1991–2020 [climate normals](42778673.md)

The [climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate) of Colorado is more complex than states outside of the [Mountain States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mountain_States) region. Unlike most other states, southern Colorado is not always warmer than northern Colorado. Most of Colorado is made up of mountains, foothills, high plains, and desert lands. Mountains and surrounding valleys greatly affect the local climate. Northeast, east, and southeast Colorado are mostly the high plains, while Northern Colorado is a mix of high plains, foothills, and mountains. Northwest and west Colorado are predominantly mountainous, with some desert lands mixed in. Southwest and southern Colorado are a complex mixture of desert and mountain areas.

### Eastern Plains

The climate of the Eastern Plains is [semi-arid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semi-arid_climate) ([Köppen climate classification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification): [*BSk*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cold_semi-arid_climate)) with low humidity and moderate precipitation, usually from 15 to 25 inches (380 to 640 mm) annually, although many areas near the rivers are semi-humid climate. The area is known for its abundant sunshine and cool, clear nights, which give this area a great average [diurnal temperature range](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diurnal_temperature_variation). The difference between the highs of the days and the lows of the nights can be considerable as warmth dissipates to space during clear nights, the heat radiation not being trapped by clouds. The Front Range urban corridor, where most of the population of Colorado resides, lies in a pronounced [precipitation shadow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rain_shadow) as a result of being on the [lee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leeward) side of the Rocky Mountains.[^cite_note-usgsFRUC1978-112]

In summer, this area can have many days above 95 °F (35 °C) and often 100 °F (38 °C).[^cite_note-Climate_of_Colorado-113] On the plains, the winter lows usually range from 25 to −10 °F (−4 to −23 °C). About 75% of the precipitation falls within the growing season, from April to September, but this area is very prone to droughts. Most of the precipitation comes from thunderstorms, which can be severe, and from major snowstorms that occur in the winter and early spring. Otherwise, winters tend to be mostly dry and cold.[^cite_note-Denver-Colorado-United-States-of-America-114]

In much of the region, March is the snowiest month. April and May are normally the rainiest months, while April is the wettest month overall. The Front Range cities closer to the mountains tend to be warmer in the winter due to [Chinook winds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinook_wind) which warm the area, sometimes bringing temperatures of 70 °F (21 °C) or higher in the winter.[^cite_note-Denver-Colorado-United-States-of-America-114] The average July temperature is 55 °F (13 °C) in the morning and 90 °F (32 °C) in the afternoon. The average January temperature is 18 °F (−8 °C) in the morning and 48 °F (9 °C) in the afternoon, although variation between consecutive days can be 40 °F (4 °C).

### Front Range foothills

Just west of the plains and into the foothills, there is a wide variety of climate types. Locations merely a few miles apart can experience entirely different weather depending on the topography. Most valleys have a semi-arid climate, not unlike the eastern plains, which transitions to an [alpine climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alpine_climate) at the highest elevations. Microclimates also exist in local areas that run nearly the entire spectrum of climates, including subtropical highland (*Cfb/Cwb*), humid subtropical (*Cfa*), humid continental (*Dfa/Dfb*), Mediterranean (*Csa/Csb*) and subarctic (*Dfc*).[^cite_note-115]

### Extreme weather

Extreme weather changes are common in Colorado, although a significant portion of the extreme weather occurs in the least populated areas of the state. Thunderstorms are common east of the Continental Divide in the spring and summer, yet are usually brief. Hail is a common sight in the mountains east of the Divide and across the eastern Plains, especially the northeast part of the state. Hail is the most commonly reported warm-season severe weather hazard, and occasionally causes human injuries, as well as significant property damage.[^cite_note-Childs-116] The eastern Plains are subject to some of the biggest hail storms in North America.[^cite_note-ccc-77] Notable examples are the severe hailstorms that hit Denver on July 11, 1990,[^cite_note-117] and May 8, 2017, the latter being the costliest ever in the state.[^cite_note-118]

The Eastern Plains are part of the extreme western portion of [Tornado Alley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tornado_Alley); some damaging tornadoes in the Eastern Plains include the 1990 [Limon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Limon,_Colorado) [F3](165206.md) tornado and the [2008 Windsor EF3 tornado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tornado_outbreak_of_May_22%E2%80%9327,_2008#Windsor,_Colorado), which devastated a small town.[^cite_note-119] Portions of the eastern Plains see especially frequent tornadoes, both those spawned from [mesocyclones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mesocyclone) in [supercell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supercell) thunderstorms and from less intense [landspouts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Landspout), such as within the [Denver convergence vorticity zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Denver_convergence_vorticity_zone) (DCVZ).[^cite_note-Childs-116]

The Plains are also susceptible to occasional floods and particularly severe flash floods, which are caused both by thunderstorms and by the rapid melting of snow in the mountains during warm weather. Notable examples include the [1965 Denver Flood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1965_Denver_Flood?action=edit\&redlink=1),[^cite_note-120] the [Big Thompson River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Big_Thompson_River) flooding of 1976 and the [2013 Colorado floods](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2013_Colorado_floods). Hot weather is common during summers in Denver. The city's record in 1901 for the number of consecutive days above 90 °F (32 °C) was broken during the summer of 2008. The new record of 24 consecutive days surpassed the previous record by almost a week.[^cite_note-121]

Much of Colorado is very dry, with the state averaging only 17 inches (430 mm) of precipitation per year statewide. The state rarely experiences a time when some portion is not in some degree of drought.[^cite_note-122] The lack of precipitation contributes to the severity of wildfires in the state, such as the [Hayman Fire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hayman_Fire) of 2002. Other notable fires include the [Fourmile Canyon Fire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fourmile_Canyon#Fourmile_Canyon_Fire) of 2010, the [Waldo Canyon Fire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waldo_Canyon_Fire) and [High Park Fire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High_Park_Fire) of June 2012, and the [Black Forest Fire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Forest_Fire) of June 2013. Even these fires were exceeded in severity by the [Pine Gulch Fire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pine_Gulch_Fire), [Cameron Peak Fire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cameron_Peak_Fire), and [East Troublesome Fire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Troublesome_Fire) in 2020, all being the three largest fires in Colorado history (see [2020 Colorado wildfires](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Colorado_wildfires)). And the Marshall Fire which started on December 30, 2021, while not the largest in state history, was the most destructive ever in terms of property loss (see [Marshall Fire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marshall_Fire)).

However, some of the mountainous regions of Colorado receive a huge amount of moisture from winter snowfalls. The spring melts of these snows often cause great waterflows in the [Yampa River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yampa_River), the [Colorado River](180968.md), the Rio Grande, the Arkansas River, the North Platte River, and the South Platte River.

Water flowing out of the Colorado Rocky Mountains is a very significant source of water for the farms, towns, and cities of the southwest states of New Mexico, Arizona, Utah, and Nevada,[^cite_note-123] as well as the Midwest, such as Nebraska[^cite_note-124] and Kansas,[^cite_note-\:7-125] and the southern states of Oklahoma[^cite_note-\:7-125] and Texas. A significant amount of water is also diverted for use in California; occasionally (formerly naturally and consistently), the flow of water reaches northern Mexico.[^cite_note-126]

### Climate change

These paragraphs are an excerpt from [Climate change in Colorado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_change_in_Colorado).\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Climate_change_in_Colorado\&action=edit)]

[Climate change in Colorado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_change_in_Colorado) encompasses the effects of [climate change](5042951.md), attributed to man-made increases in atmospheric [greenhouse gases](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greenhouse_gases), in the U.S. state of Colorado.

In 2019 *[The Denver Post](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Denver_Post)* reported that "\[i]ndividuals living in southeastern Colorado are more vulnerable to potential health effects from climate change than residents in other parts of the state".[^cite_note-127] The [United States Environmental Protection Agency](58666.md) (EPA) has more broadly reported:

"Colorado's climate is changing. Most of the state has warmed one or two degrees (F) in the last century. Throughout the [western United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_United_States), [heat waves](293270.md) are becoming more common, [snow](28191.md) is melting earlier in spring, and less water flows through the Colorado River.[^cite_note-128][^cite_note-129] Rising temperatures[^cite_note-130] and recent droughts[^cite_note-131] in the region have killed many trees by drying out soils, increasing the risk of forest fires, or enabling outbreaks of forest insects. In the coming decades, the changing climate is likely to decrease water availability and agricultural yields in Colorado, and further increase the risk of [wildfires](56106.md)".[^cite_note-Climate_change_in_Colorado_EPA-132]

### Records

The highest official ambient air temperature ever recorded in Colorado was 115 °F (46.1 °C) on July 20, 2019, at [John Martin Dam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Martin_Reservoir). The lowest official air temperature was −61 °F (−51.7 °C) on February 1, 1985, at [Maybell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maybell,_Colorado).[^cite_note-StateMaxTemps-133][^cite_note-StateMinTemps-134]

### Earthquakes

Despite its mountainous terrain, Colorado experiences less seismic activity than states like California and Alaska. There are over 90 potentially active faults, and since 1867, Colorado has experienced 700 recorded earthquakes of magnitude 2.5 or higher.[^cite_note-137] The U.S. [National Earthquake Information Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Earthquake_Information_Center) is located in [Golden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden,_Colorado).[^cite_note-138]

On August 22, 2011, a [5.3 magnitude earthquake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011_Colorado_earthquake) occurred 9 miles (14 km) west-southwest of the city of [Trinidad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trinidad,_Colorado).[^cite_note-139] There were no casualties and only a small amount of damage was reported. It was the second-largest earthquake in Colorado's history, the largest being a [magnitude 6.6 earthquake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1882_Fort_Collins_earthquake), recorded in 1882.[^cite_note-140] Four minor earthquakes rattled Colorado on August 24, 2018, ranging from magnitude 2.9 to 4.3.[^cite_note-141] As of June 2020, there were 525 recorded earthquakes in Colorado since 1973, a majority of which range 2 to 3.5 on the Richter scale.[^cite_note-142]

## Fauna

Further information on the lists of: [amphibians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_amphibians_of_Colorado), [birds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_birds_of_Colorado), [mammals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_mammals_of_Colorado), and [reptiles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_reptiles_of_Colorado)

Photo of Breckenridge naturalist Edwin Carter standing next to a taxidermied gray wolf killed in the Colorado Rockies, c. 1890–1900

Breckenridge naturalist [Edwin Carter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edwin_Carter) with a mounted [gray wolf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gray_wolf) killed in the Colorado Rockies, c. 1890–1900

A process of extirpation by trapping and poisoning of the [gray wolf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gray_wolf) (*Canis lupus*) from Colorado in the 1930s saw the last wild wolf in the state shot in 1945.[^cite_note-SierraWolf-143] A [wolf pack recolonized Moffat County, Colorado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Repopulation_of_wolves_in_Colorado) in northwestern Colorado in 2019.[^cite_note-144] Cattle farmers have expressed concern that a returning wolf population potentially threatens their herds.[^cite_note-SierraWolf-143] Coloradans [voted to reintroduce gray wolves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Colorado_Proposition_114) in 2020, with the state committing to a plan to have a population in the state by 2022 and permitting non-lethal methods of driving off wolves attacking livestock and pets.[^cite_note-145][^cite_note-146]

While there is fossil evidence of [Harrington's mountain goat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harrington's_mountain_goat) in Colorado between at least 800,000 years ago and its extinction with [megafauna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Megafauna) roughly 11,000 years ago, the [mountain goat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mountain_goat) is not native to Colorado but was instead introduced to the state over time during the interval between 1947 and 1972. Despite being an artificially-introduced species, the state declared mountain goats a native species in 1993.[^cite_note-147] In 2013, 2014, and 2019, an unknown illness killed nearly all mountain goat kids, leading to a [Colorado Parks and Wildlife](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colorado_Parks_and_Wildlife) investigation.[^cite_note-148][^cite_note-149]

The native population of [pronghorn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pronghorn) in Colorado has varied wildly over the last century, reaching a low of only 15,000 individuals during the 1960s. However, conservation efforts succeeded in bringing the stable population back up to roughly 66,000 by 2013.[^cite_note-150] The population was estimated to have reached 85,000 by 2019 and had increasingly more run-ins with the increased suburban housing along the eastern Front Range. State wildlife officials suggested that landowners would need to modify fencing to allow the greater number of pronghorns to move unabated through the newly developed land.[^cite_note-151] Pronghorns are most readily found in the northern and eastern portions of the state, with some populations also in the western San Juan Mountains.[^cite_note-152]

Common wildlife found in the mountains of Colorado include [mule deer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mule_deer), [southwestern red squirrel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southwestern_red_squirrel), [golden-mantled ground squirrel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden-mantled_ground_squirrel), [yellow-bellied marmot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yellow-bellied_marmot), [moose](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moose), [American pika](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_pika), and [red fox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_fox), all at exceptionally high numbers, though moose are not native to the state.[^cite_note-153][^cite_note-154][^cite_note-155][^cite_note-156] The foothills include [deer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deer), [fox squirrel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fox_squirrel), [desert cottontail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Desert_cottontail), [mountain cottontail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mountain_cottontail), and [coyote](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coyote).[^cite_note-157][^cite_note-158] The prairies are home to [black-tailed prairie dog](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black-tailed_prairie_dog), the endangered [swift fox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swift_fox), [American badger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_badger), and [white-tailed jackrabbit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White-tailed_jackrabbit).[^cite_note-159][^cite_note-160][^cite_note-161]

## Government

Main article: [Government of Colorado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_of_Colorado)

### State government

| Office | Name | Party |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Governor | [Jared Polis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jared_Polis) | Democratic |
| Lieutenant Governor | [Dianne Primavera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dianne_Primavera) | Democratic |
| Secretary of State | [Jena Griswold](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jena_Griswold) | Democratic |
| Attorney General | [Phil Weiser](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phil_Weiser) | Democratic |
| Treasurer | [Dave Young](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dave_Young_\(Colorado_politician\)) | Democratic |

Like the federal government and all other U.S. states, Colorado's [state constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_the_State_of_Colorado) provides for three branches of government: the legislative, the executive, and the judicial branches.[^cite_note-162]

The [Governor of Colorado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governor_of_Colorado) heads the state's executive branch. The current governor is [Jared Polis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jared_Polis), a [Democrat](5043544.md). Colorado's other statewide elected executive officers are the [Lieutenant Governor of Colorado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lieutenant_Governor_of_Colorado) (elected on a [ticket](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ticket_\(election\)) with the Governor), [Secretary of State of Colorado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secretary_of_State_of_Colorado), [Colorado State Treasurer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colorado_State_Treasurer), and [Attorney General of Colorado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attorney_General_of_Colorado), all of whom serve four-year terms.[^cite_note-163]

The seven-member [Colorado Supreme Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colorado_Supreme_Court) is the state's highest court. The [Colorado Court of Appeals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colorado_Court_of_Appeals), with 22 judges, sits in divisions of three judges each. Colorado is divided into 23 judicial districts, each of which has a district court and a county court with limited jurisdiction. The state also has [specialized water courts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colorado_water_courts), which sit in seven distinct divisions around the state and which decide matters relating to water rights and the use and administration of water.[^cite_note-164]

The state legislative body is the [Colorado General Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colorado_General_Assembly), which is made up of two houses – the [House of Representatives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colorado_House_of_Representatives) and the [Senate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colorado_Senate). The House has 65 members and the Senate has 35. As of April 2026, the [Democratic Party](5043544.md) holds a 23 to 12 majority in the Senate and a 43 to 22 majority in the House.[^cite_note-165][^cite_note-166]

Most Coloradans are native to other states (nearly 60% according to the 2000 census),[^cite_note-167] and this is illustrated by the fact that the state did not have a native-born governor from 1975 (when [John David Vanderhoof](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_David_Vanderhoof) left office) until 2007, when [Bill Ritter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bill_Ritter) took office; [his election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2006_Colorado_gubernatorial_election) the previous year marked the first electoral victory for a native-born Coloradan in a gubernatorial race since 1958 (Vanderhoof had ascended from the Lieutenant Governorship when [John Arthur Love](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Arthur_Love) was given a position in [Richard Nixon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Nixon)'s administration in 1973).

State sales taxes and many state-collected local sales taxes are administered and collected by the [Colorado Department of Revenue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colorado_Department_of_Revenue).[^cite_note-168]

Colorado was once considered a [swing state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swing_state), but has become a relatively safe [blue state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_states_and_blue_states) in both state and federal elections since the late 2010s. In presidential elections, it had not been won until [2020](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_United_States_presidential_election_in_Colorado) by double digits since [1984](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1984_United_States_presidential_election_in_Colorado) and has backed the winning candidate in 9 of the last 11 elections. Coloradans have elected 17 [Democrats](5043544.md) and 12 [Republicans](32070.md) to the governorship in the last 100 years.

In presidential politics, Colorado was considered a reliably Republican state during the post-World War II era, voting for the Democratic candidate only in 1948, 1964, and 1992. However, it became a competitive swing state in the 1990s. Since the mid-2000s, it has swung heavily to the Democrats, voting for [Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama) in 2008[^cite_note-170] and 2012,[^cite_note-171] [Hillary Clinton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hillary_Clinton) in 2016,[^cite_note-172] [Joe Biden](145422.md) in 2020,[^cite_note-173] and [Kamala Harris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kamala_Harris) in 2024.[^cite_note-174]

Colorado politics exhibits a contrast between conservative cities such as Colorado Springs and Grand Junction, and liberal cities such as Boulder and Denver. Democrats are strongest in [metropolitan Denver](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Denver_Metropolitan_Area), the college towns of [Fort Collins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fort_Collins,_Colorado) and [Boulder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boulder,_Colorado), southern Colorado (including Pueblo), and several western ski resort counties. The Republicans are strongest in the Eastern Plains, Colorado Springs, Greeley, and far Western Colorado near Grand Junction.

Colorado is represented by two members of the [United States Senate](24909346.md):[^cite_note-175]

- [Class 2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classes_of_United_States_senators#Class_2), [John Hickenlooper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Hickenlooper) ([Democratic](5043544.md)), since 2021

- [Class 3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classes_of_United_States_senators#Class_3), [Michael Bennet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Bennet) ([Democratic](5043544.md)), since 2009

Colorado is represented by eight members of the [United States House of Representatives](19468510.md):[^cite_note-176]

- [1st district](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colorado's_1st_congressional_district): [Diana DeGette](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diana_DeGette) ([Democratic](5043544.md)), since 1997

- [2nd district](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colorado's_2nd_congressional_district): [Joe Neguse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joe_Neguse) ([Democratic](5043544.md)), since 2019

- [3rd district](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colorado's_3rd_congressional_district): [Jeff Hurd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeff_Hurd_\(politician\)) ([Republican](32070.md)), since 2025

- [4th district](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colorado's_4th_congressional_district): [Lauren Boebert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lauren_Boebert) ([Republican](32070.md)), since 2025 (3rd district 2021–2025)

- [5th district](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colorado's_5th_congressional_district): [Jeff Crank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeff_Crank) ([Republican](32070.md)), since 2025

- [6th district](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colorado's_6th_congressional_district): [Jason Crow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jason_Crow) ([Democratic](5043544.md)), since 2019

- [7th district](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colorado's_7th_congressional_district): [Brittany Pettersen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brittany_Pettersen) ([Democratic](5043544.md)), since 2023

- [8th district](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colorado's_8th_congressional_district): [Gabe Evans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gabe_Evans) ([Republican](32070.md)), since 2025

In a 2020 study, Colorado was ranked as the seventh easiest state for citizens to vote in.[^cite_note-177]

#### Significant initiatives and legislation enacted in Colorado

Colorado was the first state in the union to enact, by voter referendum, [a law extending](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1893_Colorado_women's_suffrage_referendum) [suffrage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suffrage) to women. That initiative was approved by the state's voters on November 7, 1893.[^cite_note-178]

On the November 8, 1932, ballot, Colorado approved the [repeal of alcohol prohibition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Repeal_of_Prohibition) more than a year before the [Twenty-first Amendment to the United States Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twenty-first_Amendment_to_the_United_States_Constitution) was ratified.

Colorado has banned, via C.R.S. section 12-6-302, the sale of motor vehicles on Sunday since at least 1953.[^cite_note-179]

In 1972, Colorado voters rejected a [referendum proposal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1972_Denver_Winter_Olympics_referendum) to fund the [1976 Winter Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1976_Winter_Olympics), which had been scheduled to be held in the state. Denver had been chosen by the [International Olympic Committee](15147.md) as the host city on May 12, 1970.[^cite_note-180]

In 1992, by a margin of 53 to 47 percent, Colorado voters approved an amendment to the state constitution (Amendment 2) that would have prevented any city, town, or county in the state from taking any legislative, executive, or judicial action to recognize homosexuals or bisexuals as a [protected class](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protected_class).[^cite_note-181] In 1996, in a 6–3 ruling in *[Romer v. Evans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romer_v._Evans)*, the U.S. Supreme Court found that preventing protected status based upon homosexuality or [bisexuality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bisexuality) did not satisfy the [Equal Protection Clause](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Equal_Protection_Clause).[^cite_note-romervevans-182]

In 2006, voters passed [Amendment 43](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2006_Colorado_Amendment_43), which banned [same-sex marriage in Colorado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Same-sex_marriage_in_Colorado).[^cite_note-183] That initiative was nullified by the [U.S. Supreme Court](31737.md)'s 2015 decision in *[Obergefell v. Hodges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Obergefell_v._Hodges)*. In 2024, Colorado residents voted to establish an explicit right to [abortion in Colorado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abortion_in_Colorado)'s state constitution[^cite_note-184][^cite_note-185] and to repeal Amendment 43's defunct marriage ban.[^cite_note-186][^cite_note-187]

In 2012, voters amended the state constitution protecting the "personal use" of marijuana for adults, establishing a framework to regulate [cannabis](1481886.md) like [alcohol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alcoholic_beverage). The first recreational marijuana shops in Colorado, and by extension the United States, opened their doors on January 1, 2014.[^cite_note-Healy-188]

On October 30, 2019, Colorado became the first state to accept digital ID via its myColorado app.[^cite_note-189] The state-issued digital identifications will be considered valid when Real ID enforcement begins in 2025, in line with the [Real ID Act of 2005](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Real_ID_Act). By November 2022 The Colorado Governor's Office of Information Technology announced that the myColorado app had over 1 million users.[^cite_note-190]

On December 19, 2023, the [Colorado Supreme Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colorado_Supreme_Court) ruled that [Donald Trump](4848272.md) was disqualified from the [2024 United States presidential election](59568546.md) in part due to his alleged incitement of the [January 6 United States Capitol attack](66307490.md).[^cite_note-191] On March 4, 2024, the [United States Supreme Court](31737.md) [overruled](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trump_v._Anderson) the Colorado decision.[^cite_note-192]

The [64 counties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_counties_in_Colorado) of the [U.S.](3434750.md) [State of Colorado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_of_Colorado)

Main article: [List of counties in Colorado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_counties_in_Colorado)

The State of Colorado is divided into 64 [counties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/County_\(United_States\)). Two of these counties, the [City and County of Broomfield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Broomfield,_Colorado) and the [City and County of Denver](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Denver), have [consolidated city and county governments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consolidated_city-county). Counties are important units of government in Colorado since there are no [civil townships](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_township) or other [minor civil divisions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minor_civil_division).[^cite_note-193]

The most populous county in Colorado is [El Paso County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/El_Paso_County,_Colorado), the home of the [City of Colorado Springs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colorado_Springs,_Colorado). The second most populous county is the [City and County of Denver](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Denver), the state capital. Five of the 64 counties now have more than 500,000 residents, while 12 have fewer than 5,000 residents. The ten most populous Colorado counties are all located in the [Front Range Urban Corridor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Front_Range_Urban_Corridor). [Mesa County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mesa_County,_Colorado) is the most populous county on the [Colorado Western Slope](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colorado_Western_Slope). The least populous Colorado county is [Hinsdale County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinsdale_County,_Colorado) with 753 residents.[^cite_note-2025_county_estimates-195]
